I’ll suddenly just feel like I can't sit there anymore, so I end up making an excuse to get up for water or to go to the printer. It’s definitely not a conscious choice where I decide to just ignore what I'm supposed to do, it's just... I find myself doing it without really thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Are these incidents of getting up during meetings causing you a lot of stress or problems at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it actually is. Um, it's started showing up in my performance reviews, which is pretty stressful. And... well, it seems to be affecting client trust, too. There was this one client complaint recently that really made me worry. I realized I could actually damage my accounts, which is incredibly frustrating because I really do care a lot about doing good work. It's just getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless when you're sitting in those meetings before you get up?\n\nPatient: Um, not really? I wouldn't say I feel restless before I get up. It's more like... I just realize I'm already getting up. I don't really sit there feeling antsy or anything, it just sort of happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or thoughts over and over outside of these meetings?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I don't think I do anything like that. I haven't noticed myself repeating actions or getting stuck on specific thoughts over and over. It's really just the issue with staying seated and losing focus, but nothing repetitive like you're describing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried about things in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I mean, obviously I get stressed about work sometimes—like with the performance reviews I just mentioned—but outside of that? No. I wouldn't say I'm an anxious person, and I don't really find myself worrying about things in my day-to-day life.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to perform certain actions in a specific way or repeat them?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I don't have any routines or actions that I feel like I have to do in a certain way. It's really just the trouble focusing and staying in my seat.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

My mind jumps around a lot more than it gets stuck on one thing, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have unwanted thoughts that keep coming back into your mind?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I generally know what I'm doing and weigh the options, especially with anything important.
